The bake now runs at a **fixed 1 canvas px per world px**, so zoom cannot re-key anything: chunks are baked once per map and the blit scales them, and the coarse stand-in's first bake of a frame is no longer starved by the pixel budget (a starved cover pass was the other half of the gap patchwork — the void showed as chunk-shaped rectangles at 11 fps on a 2×-spec map). `groundWorldStepForZoom` is deleted with it. **And the decor overlay is gone as a whole-map surface:** `bakeTerrainDecor`, `TerrainDecorCache`, `terrainDecorNeedsRebuild` and `disposeTerrainDecor` are deleted, and the props and river courses bake **into the ground chunk that holds them** (`renderer/decor.bakeDecorInRect`, with a 32 px overhang margin and an origin rule so each prop is painted exactly once — no seams, no double-draw). That is the prerequisite for the spec sizes: the old overlay was 4.3 MB at 1200×900 but **113 MB at 6144×4608**, and it was the last full-map surface in the ground path. **Also:** the land screen's chooser is now a square bordered panel (`MapSetupScreen`) instead of floating on the full-bleed gradient. **Attempted and reverted the same session:** raising `MAP_SIZE_DIMENSIONS` to the Teraforge spec sizes (2560×1920 / 4096×3072 / 6144×4608). The attempt is recorded in that constant's comment with the evidence, because it failed its own gates — the 50 % overview of a 2560×1920 world rendered as chunk gaps at 11 fps at the then-current bake step, and four suites encode the shipped scale (`saveWorldMap.compact`, `terragen.riverGeneration`, `medium-C1-economy`, `workCommute.leadHour`). The order that works is recorded there: chunk the decor (**done**), then give each chunk a zoom-following bake step re-baked in place, then raise the sizes and re-pin those four suites. **Verified:** `npm run test:types` exit 0 · `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors · `npm run build` exit 0 · `npm run test:standard` **242 files / 1482 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed** · `tests/decorProps.tint.test.ts` 3/3 (rewritten for the per-chunk bake: no canvas filter, the shading itself, and the origin rule) · `tests/groundChunk.bands.test.ts` 3/3 · `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` 9/9 · `scripts/browser-smoke.mjs` `verdict=pass` at both the shipped and the spec sizes, `distinctColors` 15,184 with the props in the per-chunk bakes. **Two audits are on file for the next session:** `docs/private/audits/2026-09-29/terrain-rendering.md` (twelve findings in the terrain render path, with what is fixed and what is open) and `docs/private/audits/2026-09-29/godot-port-assessment.md` (a measured, two-sided read on porting to Godot — code inventory, what Godot replaces, what must be rewritten, the 2.5D/3D presentation routes, and the sprite inventory: 110 of 214 files actually referenced).

- **Fixed: the L3 ground props are baked into the decor overlay once per map instead of being re-drawn as vector art every frame — the last big cost in the frame** — reported from play as *"running at 14 fps"*, with the simulation paused (so: render-bound, not sim-bound) and a hardware-accelerated canvas at 1920×1280. A CPU profile of one built session (drag pan, **dev** build, so the names are real) put `drawProp`, `tinted` and the `save`/`restore`/`translate`/`scale` calls plus most of the `stroke`/`fill`/`arc`/`ellipse` time at **~35 % of main-thread work** — over **6,220 props on a Medium map** (36,110 on Huge) with every prop in view re-drawn each frame, and every one of them static since `terragen.placeDecorations` writes them at generation. They now go **into the decor overlay**, which already held the river stroke and the map frame and was already cached per map: `renderer/decor.bakeDecorProps` draws them once, over the river stroke, in the order the frame used to draw them, and the per-frame prop pass is gone. The overlay is baked at **2 canvas px per world px** for maps up to 8 Mpx (Medium and Large; Huge stays 1:1, where it already was) because it is blitted at `worldW × zoom`, and the two baked layers are now blitted with **smoothing on** — they are continuous art, and the frame's `imageSmoothingEnabled = false` is for the entity sprites, which is what stair-stepped the shader's fine detail and the props above 100 % zoom. **Measured in the same dev session, same viewport:** stationary **p50 143.5 ms → 26.2 ms**, zoom sweeps **100.4 ms → 14.5 ms**, drag pan **89.5 ms → 17.4 ms**, and no frame over 500 ms in any window; `drawProp` and `tinted` no longer appear in the frame profile at all. **What is on top now:** `drawWaterShimmer` (18 % of remaining main-thread work — a per-water-tile pass that builds four `rgba()` strings per tile per frame), the two baked blits (13 %) and `tileAt` (4 %, the minimap's 1,728 samples every fifth frame). Gates: `npm run test:types` exit 0, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0, `npm run test:standard` **242 files / 1482 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**, `tests/decorProps.tint.test.ts` 3/3 (no canvas filter, the shading itself, and an empty prop list baking nothing), `scripts/browser-smoke.mjs` `verdict=pass` (`distinctColors` 14,702 — the props are in the baked overlay and visibly on screen).

- **Fixed: the terrain no longer stalls the game — the decor pass stopped writing a canvas filter per prop, and the ground bake is now bounded per frame instead of baked in one blocking slice** — reported from play as *"the game is not running, 0 fps … the game stalls at 0 fps … i think of the new terrain layer"*. Two independent defects were behind it, and **instrumenting the built game is what separated them**: with the camera still, Chrome at 1280×720, the page measured **p50 6,288 ms per frame and 0 ground chunks baked in a 10 s window** — so the freeze was not the Whittaker ground bake at all. **(1) The decor pass wrote a canvas filter per prop, every frame.** `renderer/decor.ts` `drawProp` set `ctx.filter = 'brightness(…)'` for each prop, and a filtered 2D draw is its own software-composited pass; the scale is not small — a Medium map holds **6,220** decor props with **4,283 of them inside one 1600×900 window**, and `terragen.placeDecorations` gives **every** prop a non-zero tint (`(hash2(gx, gy, seed) - 0.5) * 2`). A/B in one live session, same camera and world: **p50 6,288 ms** as shipped · **p50 109 ms** with the filter writes dropped · **p50 3,805 ms** with them restored. The brightness variation is now applied to the prop's own colours (`tinted(color, level)`, 19 levels, memoised, the same `1 ± 0.18`), so the vector art stays crisp at every zoom and no filtered draw happens at all. **(2) The ground bake blocked the frame it happened in.** `renderer/terrain.ts` baked every missing 256² chunk in one frame; a chunk measures **100–300 ms** of per-pixel shader work and a fresh view at the default zoom is 20–24 of them — **3.9–6.0 s**, which is what a session start, a zoom-preset change and *every season rollover* paid (season is part of the chunk key). The fill is now **budgeted**: 8,192 px of new ground per frame, written into a chunk in horizontal bands, which is pixel-identical to the whole-chunk bake it replaces (pinned by `tests/groundChunk.bands.test.ts`, at world steps 1 and 2). Because a budget that small would leave the player looking at the void for seconds, each frame runs **two passes**: a *cover* pass gives every planned chunk one coarse stand-in bake at `step × 4` (1/16 the pixels, ~6–19 ms) before any of them is sharpened, then a *sharpen* pass finishes chunks in view-centre-outward order — so the ground arrives almost immediately and sharpens in place, and a season change (which keeps the chunk grid and only moves the palette) keeps the previous image on screen while the new rows are written over it rather than flashing empty. Chunk surfaces now go through the canvas owner (`canvasLayer.createCanvasSurface` / `disposeCanvasSurface`) and are released on prune, key change and reset, which the walk previously leaked. **Measured after, built game, 1280×720, from the moment the valley opens:** the fill window runs **96 frames with p50 52 ms and max 78 ms — no frame over 500 ms** — the ground already painted (luma 133.5, 13,614 distinct colours), the fill completes inside the first ten seconds at this viewport in a **GPU-less** headless browser (196 frames / p50 51 ms / **zero bakes** once it is done); **worst frame 6,288 ms → 78 ms**. Same session, the browser smoke gate is green (`verdict=pass`: ground painted, clock advancing, 50 % zoom control live, no console/page/request faults). **And the ground is no longer seasonal at all.** The bake applied a per-season tint inline — a vegetation-weighted shift (spring green, summer yellow, fall rust) and winter snow settling with height — which made `season` part of the chunk cache key, so **every season rollover re-baked the whole visible view, four times an in-game year, for a palette change**. That block and its helpers (`vegetationWeight`, `WINTER_SNOW_LINE`) are deleted, and `bakeWhittakerGround` no longer takes a season: the ground is a function of the map and the bake resolution alone, so a chunk is baked once and a season costs nothing (owner decision, 2026-09-29 — the seasonal ground look was traded away for the cost, not fixed). Seasons remain visible everywhere else they already were: grass colour and density per season (`renderer/grass.ts`), the weather layer, the HUD season readout, and the simulation's own seasonal rules (grass growth 1.8/1.2/0.7/0.35 spring→winter, reproduction 1.4/1.0/0.8/0.5, winter energy penalty, farm output 0.6 in winter and 1.3 in summer, no blueberry foraging in winter). **Two stale things went with it:** `terrainLayer.terrainLayerNeedsRebuild` had no call site and is deleted, and the decor overlay's cache key carried `getTerrainRevision()`, so clearing forest to place a building re-allocated and re-stroked a full-map canvas (4.3 MB on Medium, 19.7 MB on Huge) for a layer that reads only rivers and the map frame — the term is gone; and `renderer/terrain.ts` carried its own copy of `terrainGrid.hasContinuousFields`, which is now the one owner. `TerrainDecoration` in `gameTypes` is the one declaration of the L3 prop shape (it was declared three times, in `gameTypes`, `terragen` and `decor`). A read-only audit of the whole terrain render path — including what was **not** fixed — is `docs/private/audits/2026-09-29/terrain-rendering.md`. Gates: `npm run test:types` exit 0, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0, `npm run test:standard` **242 files / 1481 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**, `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` 9/9, `scripts/browser-smoke.mjs` `verdict=pass`. **Left open, on purpose:** the shipping ground still has no seasonal *blend* (`seasonBlendForDay` is called only by the retired tile path, so a season change swaps rather than fades) and the ground is still blitted with `imageSmoothingEnabled = false`, i.e. nearest-neighbour, at any zoom above 1 — both are art-direction calls, both are recorded in the audit rather than changed here.

- **Fixed: every preset was forced to contain every biome — each AoE2 archetype now has its own composition** — the presets are Age of Empires II map archetypes, and an AoE2 map is a **region**, not a hemisphere: Arabia is open desert, Black Forest is a wall of trees, Oasis is desert with a pool. Two mechanisms were overriding that and giving all ten presets one shape. **(1) Latitude cooling was global.** `LATITUDE_SPAN = 0.3` was applied to every preset, so every map froze at its north and south rim — measured, `arabia` carried 5.0 % tundra and `meadows` (a "flat European lowland") came out 26 % taiga. The span is now **per preset**, taken from the preset's own climate intent: **0** for the eight regional archetypes and a gradient only for `scandinavia` (the cold Nordic map) and `continental` (the deliberately mixed one). `arabia` tundra **5.0 % → 0.8 %**, its desert biome **11.3 % → 25.4 %**. **(2) The forest cut was two fixed quantiles for every map.** `MOISTURE_FOREST_QUANTILE = 0.58` / `0.86` meant each map grew the same canopy share whatever its climate — `arabia` (hot, semi-arid) and `oasis` (desert) each had **a third to a half of their tiles as forest**, while the L2 biome field, which reads the preset's own `forest`, called those same maps `dirt 54 % / desert 36 %`. Two classifiers disagreeing about the same ground. The quantile now follows the preset's `forest` want (`lerp(0.97, 0.05, forest)`), so composition is authored in one place; the quantile itself still comes from the map's own moisture distribution, because an absolute cut paints one preset solid grass and the next solid canopy. `forest` is now the knob it reads as, and the two desert maps are authored low: `arabia` **0.3 → 0.08**, `oasis` **0.15 → 0.06**. Measured forest tiles: **arabia 24 % → 8.9 %**, **oasis 15.4 % → 8.4 %**, while `black_forest` went **39 % → 56.6 %** and its dense-forest biome **33.7 % → 55.4 %**, and `oasis`'s desert biome **36.1 % → 65.6 %**. **Verified:** `npm run test:standard` **240 files / 1476 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**, `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` 9/9, `tsc` clean, `npm run lint` 0/0, bake unchanged at 4.5 s whole-map / ~27–45 ms per 256 px chunk. **Left open:** `meadows` still runs 56 % forest against its "flat European lowland, lush" character. **And `TerrainType.Desert` is now a real terrain type.** The classifier's hot-and-dry corner (`te > DESERT_TEMPERATURE && mo < dryCut + deficit`) had returned `Grassland`, so an Arabian map read as pasture to everything that consults the tile — the minimap dot, the footstep surface, the fallback tile bake — while the per-pixel ground art painted it sand: two layers disagreeing about the same ground. `gameTypes.TerrainType` gains `Desert` and the corner returns it, and `desert` is now the **largest** tile type on both desert archetypes: **arabia 36.6 %** (was 0, hidden inside `grassland`'s 49.2 %) and **oasis 46.9 %**, with **continental 7.0 %** (was 0). Every consumer took its entry: `TERRAIN_PALETTE` `0xd6bd74` (the exhaustive `Record<TerrainType, number>`, so `tsc` enforced completeness), the sand fill, the `'sand'` transition family and a low relief value in the fallback tile bake, `atlasFamily -> GRASS` so an arid cell does not make its neighbours unpaintable (`pickAtlasTile` refuses a tile with an out-of-family neighbour, so returning null there would have stopped an Arabian map's whole shoreline from painting), and building efficiency (farm **0.45** — dry soil; lumber mill **0.7** — no canopy). Desert is walkable and buildable exactly like the grassland it replaced, so no placement rule moves. `meadows` is the only remaining preset whose composition looks off, against its "flat European lowland, lush" character, and that is a single-value tuning decision (`forest: 0.4`). — the meadows share is a single-value tuning decision rather than a structural one.

- **Changed: the ground renderer adopts three published methods — banded bathymetric tints, the Whittaker/Ricklefs biome chart for land colour, and a multidirectional hillshade** — a review of the rendered landscape against the standalone Teraforge build found the per-pixel bake had drifted away from established practice in three places, each replaced by the standard method rather than tuned by eye. **(1) Water.** Depth was a *continuous* ramp that saturated at a hard `0.11`, which is a fraction of the real range — measured, `islands` bottoms out **0.588** below sea level, `coastal` **0.490**, `scandinavia` **0.380**, so **81 % / 78 % / 71 %** of their sea sat at one flat colour (water local contrast **0.55–1.82** against the land's 4.0–5.1). It is now **discrete deepening tints** whose ramp spans the map's own p98 depth — how charts actually draw depth ([IHO S-4](https://iho.int/uploads/user/pubs/standards/s-4/S4_V4-9-0_March_2021.pdf)) and how `gdaldem color-relief -exact_color_entry` renders a DEM. The invented `sin(depth * 190) * 5` contour term is gone: it was not a standard method and its 2 % swing was invisible on every water pixel. **(2) Land colour** was keyed on **height** with climate as sub-branches, so every inland stop on the ramp was a shade of green and an arid preset could not reach sand — `arabia` (moisture 0.12) rendered green. Colour is now read off the **Whittaker diagram in the published Ricklefs palette** (`plotbiomes::Whittaker_biomes`, digitised from Ricklefs 2008 *The Economy of Nature* Fig. 5.5), which is the chart `classifyTile` is already modelled on, so the ground and the tile under it cannot disagree. Measured mean land colour on `arabia`: **121,143,96 → 157,167,131**. The palette is a *categorical* scheme, so it is blended toward its own grey for use as albedo (`WHITTAKER_ALBEDO_SATURATION`) and evaluated once into a 65×65 lookup table, per Red Blob Games' prescription for biome lookup. **(3) Hillshade** used a single light azimuth, which renders any slope face parallel to it flat — measured on 1:1 crops, whole `highland` flanks were one featureless grey blob. It is now **multidirectional** (USGS Open-File 92-422; `gdaldem hillshade -multidirectional`), four azimuths with the primary (~322°) weighted double so the north-west reading survives. **Verified:** `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` **9/9 pass** (alpine painted-as-lawn 0.00–0.22 % against a 0.5 % ceiling; mean green-minus-red over alpine 0.38–1.87 against 12), hill/rock luma spread **96.8–99.6** (the project's own mountain-form metric, in the range the relief work recorded), `tsc` clean, `npm run lint` 0/0, and bakes are **faster** than before the change (arabia 4228 → 3671 ms) after an allocation fix in the new per-pixel chart sample. Artefacts: `tmp/testmap-*.png`, probe `tmp/probe-landscape.mts`. **Not done:** the chart anchors are read off the published figure, not the package's 775 digitised polygon vertices, and icy swatches still read cool on hot presets because `latitudeCooling` genuinely cools their poles below `COLD_TEMPERATURE` — a preset-tuning question, not a colour one.

- **Changed: trees draw the shipped PNG art — `tree.png` and `tree2.png` — and the procedural oak/pine module is deleted** — the tree renderer drew every non-blueberry tree from two generated canvases (`getOakSprite`/`getPineSprite` in `renderer/decorSprites.ts`) while the shipped sprites `public/sprites/tree.png` (256×248, rounded) and `public/sprites/tree2.png` (170×254, taller) were preloaded and never drawn. `renderer/trees.ts` now draws the real art — odd tree ids take the taller `tree2.png`, keeping the old oak/pine split — through the same `drawSpriteFrame` path and 0.92 bottom anchor as the blueberry landmark, and `decorSprites.ts` is removed as dead code (its only consumers were those two functions). Ripe blueberry trees are unchanged (`blueberry_tree.png`). Gates: `tsc` clean, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `tests/blueberryForaging.test.ts` passes.

- **New: rivers are one connected system that reaches the sea, and they are narrow channels instead of wide bands** — reported from play as *"why is river not just one river going through the landscape"*, and the answer was that the placement was never a network: `carveRivers` picked **N springs chosen for being far apart** (a `minDist` of 12 % of the map) and walked each one downhill, so a map grew N short disconnected squiggles. A rendered river-only diagram of `continental` showed exactly that — two or three separate squiggles, none touching the coast. **The network was always there and was being ignored.** `computeFlow` already produces the drainage field, and both `terragen`'s own test and `hydrology` document that every interior cell drains to an outlet; what was missing was *selection*. Candidates are now ranked by **the length of the river the headwater feeds**, which makes the first one the map's main trunk and every later one a tributary that begins upstream of it — and cells are claimed as they are carved, so a later branch **terminates on the trunk** instead of redrawing it. Two traps are worth recording, because each produced an empty map first: ranking by drainage area alone selects the **outlet** (walking downhill from it moves one cell and stops, so the "longest" river was the shortest), and the drainage walk terminates at the **map border**, which is not automatically coast — so a channel could be carved along a chain that leaves the edge onto elevated ground and simply *stops in a field*. Candidates are therefore traced up to their true headwater before walking down, and filtered to those whose chain actually reaches water. **Measured:** `meadows` now grows an **87-cell trunk** (longest river must exceed 40 % of the map's short side, guarded by a new case), `continental` 45 cells, and its second-longest branch now ends on the same cell as the trunk — the merge, visible in the numbers. **And the channels were too wide:** cross-sections of the water field measured them at **3–6 terrain cells (48–96 px) at the median**, a band rather than a river, against the reference look of 1–2 cells. `hydrology.CHANNEL_WIDTH_SCALE` (0.3) narrows every channel inside the width law, so each preset's `riverWidth` still expresses its relative character: now **2–3 cells (32–48 px)**. **One test was measuring the wrong thing and is corrected rather than loosened:** `groundLook.bands` matched each sampled *pixel* to its nearest 10 px *tile*, which on a carved valley wall reads the far side of the wall while naming the tile on the near side — so the number moved when the river network changed even though the colour ramp did not (0.28 % → 0.59 % against a 0.5 % ceiling). It now samples each tile over its **own footprint**, which is what the case exists to check; the ceiling is unchanged. Gates: `npm run test:all` **240 files / 1476 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**, `tsc` clean on both projects, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0.

- **New: the ground reads as a textured surface instead of a flat wash — the per-pixel variation Teraforge builds into its ground albedo is now applied here, and grass regains its blades, patches and flecks** — reported from play as *"it should look better than my screenshots"*, with a reference capture from the Teraforge build (`E:\teraforge`) beside this one. **Measured first, and the measurement corrected the diagnosis.** Rendering the shipped bake to PNG and comparing against that capture on mean saturation and **local contrast** (mean absolute luma difference between a pixel and its neighbour *d* px away — the "is there detail at this scale" number) showed saturation was never the problem (0.50–0.68 here vs 0.60 there) while contrast was: at 1 px **1.02–1.27 against 3.65**, at 3 px **2.34–2.91 against 8.21**. A control ruled out the easy explanation — upscaling our own bake 2× *halves* its fine contrast rather than raising it (0.64), so the reference's extra detail is authored ground texture, not a resampled capture. **Then reading the source found the actual cause,** and it is not a tuning value. Teraforge's `render.ts` builds a four-octave variation field (`0.4·f(0.032) + 0.3·f(0.075) + 0.2·f(0.17) + 0.1·f(0.4)`) and writes it into **every base colour ramp** (`98 + nv·22` on grass, `54 + nv·14` on woodland), then adds a deliberate *"HIGH-FREQUENCY TEXTURE per biome"* pass: directional grass streaks, coarse meadow mottling, and **discrete** dark flecks for soil showing through. Wilderfolk had reduced that field to its lowest octave and dropped the high-frequency pass — in the name of the band-limit rule from Padilla (see this file's entry below), which is the right rule *for perceived roughness* and the wrong one for this: the field's own notes record the trap ("grain finer than its lower edge reads as fizz instead of as a rougher material"). **So the change keeps the structure and restores the albedo variation:** `dv` is Teraforge's four-octave field as a 0-mean deviation, consumed by `getInlandColor`'s lowland, woodland and alpine ramps and by the sand and river colour ramps; the grass mix is now `clump + patch + streak + blades + tussock` with a thresholded **fleck** on top, and dirt gains a pebble highlight. **The first attempt at this is worth recording because it failed instructively:** raising the existing amplitudes as plain per-pixel grain produced *higher* measured contrast (D1 2.35) and looked worse — a speckled, mottled surface, because independent noise at every pixel has no blade, patch or fleck in it. That attempt was reverted, and it is why the second pass ports the source's *structure* rather than its amplitudes. **Measured after:** meadows D1 **1.27 → 2.63**, D3 **2.91 → 4.63**, distinct colours **10 377 → 13 267**; continental D1 1.12 → 1.63, D3 2.47 → 3.06; scandinavia D1 1.02 → 1.42, D3 2.34 → 2.81. **Honest limit:** the gap is narrowed, not closed (~1.4× at 3 px rather than ~2.9×) — the reference is a capture of Teraforge's own build, whose tree decor is far denser than the sparse-decor `grass` row side by side with it, and that placement difference is not ground art. Gate: `npm run test:all` **240 files / 1475 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**, `tsc` clean on both projects, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0; `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` still holds, so alpine ground is still painted as stone and a cold crest still as snow.

- **Fixed: the cast shadow was painted on the sunlit flank — the light direction was written twice and one copy had its sign flipped** — the ground bake's shadow field is supposed to record how much of the sun's path to a cell is blocked, and it recorded the opposite: the hillshade computes `light = (sx·0.6 + sy·0.8)·SHADE_GAIN`, a dot product against a light **up and to the left**, while the trace that looks for a blocker marched **down and to the right** (`x + 0.7·step`, `y + 0.8·step`) — the same heading with the sign flipped, edge guard included. So a cell counted as shadowed when the ground *down-light* of it was higher, which is the definition of the **lit** face, and the shadow subtracted light from exactly the slopes the hillshade was lighting. Measured (1600×1200, seed 12345, spring, whole-map 1:1, compared inside one altitude band so it is not a comparison of biomes): the cells the old field darkened came out **+42.6 luma brighter** than the rest on highland (**+47.8** on scandinavia) — a cast shadow that makes the ground brighter is inverted, not weak. **A second defect hid behind the first:** the blocker test was a flat minimum rise with a `1/step` falloff, which is not a sun — any higher ground up-light counted, however gentle — so as soon as the heading was corrected the same rule put **45 % of highland's land at full shadow** (p90 = 1.00) and simply dimmed the map. The light is now **one** named constant pair read by both the hillshade and the trace (`LIGHT_TOWARD_X/Y`), the trace walks toward the light for **14** cells instead of 6, and a blocker has to out-climb a **sun inclination**: `SHADOW_SUN_SLOPE` (0.035 of the elevation range per cell, chosen against the terrain's own measured up-light climb of p50 0.007–0.021, p75 0.025–0.045, p90 0.048–0.074) with a distance falloff, so only ground that out-slopes the sun casts at all. **After:** shadow coverage over land **29 % / 14 % / 18 %** above 0.05 and **11.6 % / 4.7 % / 6.1 %** above 0.5 (highland / scandinavia / continental), shadow depth **+31.1 / +36.6 / +31.3 luma** — a minority of the land, on the lee side, clearly darker. `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` gains the regression case: one tall wall on flat ground, which must shadow the ground to its east and leave the ground to its west untouched. Field build costs 9–10 ms per map (shared across every chunk); the per-pixel bake is unchanged within measurement noise. Full report: `BUG_REPORTS/2026-09-25-cast-shadow-falls-on-the-lit-flank.md`.

- **New: the ground reads altitude now — a valley sits deeper than the ground around it and a ridge stands prouder, the half of relief shading a slope field cannot carry** — the bake shaded terrain from `slopeX`/`slopeY` alone, which describes how a cell *tilts* and says nothing about where on the mountain it sits. That is why elevation barely read: a valley floor is flat, so its gradient is **zero** and it came out exactly as bright as a plain. Measured before the change, the mean luma of the highest quartile of hilly ground sat **0.5 luma** from the lowest quartile on scandinavia (highland −9.3, and that is mostly snow rather than relief). A new `WhittakerFields.relief` carries each cell's height against a blurred copy of the same field (radius 6 cells), built once per map; the bake adds it to the hillshade term through the existing warm-lit / cool-shaded path, **weighted above the lowland band** so a plain stays flat instead of being turned into a landform. **Measured** (1600×1200, seed 12345, spring, whole-map 1:1): the luma spread over hilly tiles went **79.8 → 100.7** on highland, **79.1 → 97.8** on scandinavia and **79.1 → 101.8** on continental — darker darks and lighter lights, so a mountainside reads as a lit landform rather than one mid-tone plate — with the relief field itself running p10/p90 −0.047…−0.083 / 0.058…0.099 in elevation units. The 1:1 before/after crops are `tmp/relief-before-*.png` / `tmp/relief-after-*.png`. Cost: 9–10 ms per map for the extra pass, no measurable change to the per-pixel bake.

- **Removed: the dormant Pixi/WebGL ground renderer, its switch and the `pixi.js` dependency — canvas2D is now the only ground path in the tree** — owner request, and a **reversal of the recorded 2026-09-21 decision** that the path be *"retained deliberately … not used at the moment but maybe in future"* (audit `2026-09-20` bug 5, which **CLOSED** the ~348 kB chunk cost as an accepted trade-off). That trade-off is no longer accepted: `src/game/renderer/pixiTerrain.ts` is deleted, so are three stale snapshot copies under `tmp/`, and `pixi.js` (`^8.20.1`, 12 packages) is gone from `package.json`. **Why deletion beat keeping the one-line switch,** recorded because the switch had a real argument behind it: the path had already been abandoned once for a cause the owner could see — it drew a bright vector river ribbon over pale tile water with a visible square-tile checkerboard, which the owner marked wrong — and it carried three separate defect records (a terrain container never attached to `app.stage`, a rejected WebGL init retried every frame, and that water mismatch). A renderer nothing renders, nothing reviews, and only source-text tests assert the *shape* of is not an option; it is maintenance surface with no user. This is the fix the project's own audit had already written up as option **B. Remove it** (`docs/private/audits/2026-09-20/renderer-dormant-state.md`), and `CHANGELOG.md` had already contemplated it (*"retiring it properly means deleting `pixiTerrain.ts` and its reset call, which would remove the option to switch back"*). **What changed in code:** `renderer/terrain.ts` loses the `./pixiTerrain` import, the `resetPixiTerrain()` call in `resetTerrainCaches`, the `USE_PIXI_GROUND` constant and its guarded call, so `drawGround` is now unconditionally `buildTerrainCache` + `drawProceduralGround` — the path that was already shipping, so **no player-visible change**; the doc comment there records the reversal and its reasoning. **Six test cases were deleted with the code they pinned** (`tests/debugGate.guide.test.ts`'s dormant-renderer block — the switch guard and the `pixiUnavailable` latch — and `tests/visualsAudit.renderWiring.test.ts` D7 the square relief stamp, D20 the render-clock animation, D22 the per-neighbour feathering, plus the pixi line in D12), and the now-unread `ISOMETRIC_SQUASH`, `SQUARE_TEXTURE_STAMP` and `SOFT_TRANSITION_FIRST_ONLY` patterns with them, since an unread constant is the same dead surface the module was. Each site carries a one-line note saying what was removed and when, so a reader of the audit does not go looking for a guard that was deliberately retired. **Live documentation corrected rather than left false:** `AGENTS.md` §4 and `OWNERSHIP_OVERVIEW.md` no longer name a `USE_PIXI_GROUND` that does not exist and drop the `Pixi terrain paint` owner row — the same "documented decision with no code behind it" defect the audit raised twice — and the roadmap's Theme 1 rule 3 is closed as enforced by the tree. Historical records were **deliberately not rewritten**: `CHANGELOG.md`'s own older entries, the three `BUG_REPORTS/2026-09-17-pixi-*` files and the dated audits under `docs/private/` keep their text, because a release note that gets edited whenever the code moves stops being a record of what shipped. Gates after the removal: `npm run test:all` **240 files / 1475 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed** (three fewer cases than the 1478 before it, all of them guards on the deleted module), `tsc` clean on both projects, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0.

- **New: the ground detail is band-limited to the frequencies the eye actually reads roughness in — stone has bedding, snow has drifts, and the sub-pixel grain that carried neither is trimmed** — the material detail in the ground bake was a stack of `valueNoise` octaves chosen by eye, and **every one of stone's and dirt's octaves sat below the band the visual system reads surface roughness in**. Padilla, *Mathematical models for perceived roughness of three-dimensional surface textures* (PhD thesis, Heriot-Watt 2008; *Perceived roughness of 1/f^β noise surfaces*, Vision Research 48), fits a band-pass over the psychophysics of 1/f^β noise surfaces and finds perceived roughness is carried by a **narrow band**: the optimised box filter lands on **1.53–4.58 cycles per degree** (§7.3.1), and detail outside it barely moves perceived roughness. On the display calibrated for those experiments (0.255 mm pixel pitch at 87.7 cm, i.e. 60 px per degree) the band is **≈13–39 px per cycle**. Measured against the old mix, that made a concrete, visible defect: stone's fracture octaves sat at **0.6–4.5 px** periods and dirt's at **0.8–2.9 px**, so a 1:1 crop of a rocky hillside was a **uniform grey speckle with no bedding in it at all** and a snowfield was flat white — the only two materials with anything inside the band were grass (a 21 px clump) and sand (a 33 px dune). The bake now names the band (`ROUGHNESS_BAND_PX`) and the octave each material carries inside it (`ROUGHNESS_OCTAVE_PX`) — stone a bedding ledge plus rubble, dirt a patch, snow a wind-worked drift, grass a tussock, sand a wind ripple — and authors those octaves through `bandDetail(wx, wy, seed, periodPx, pxStep)`, whose period is fixed in **canvas** px so a coarse chunk bake re-samples the same structure instead of pushing it out of the band (a world-fixed octave halves its period when the world step doubles; this one does not). The sub-pixel octaves are **trimmed rather than deleted**, because they still carry material identity at close zoom: dirt's grain 26 → 18 and its 0.8 px term 10 → 5, stone's grain 20 → 8, grass's 10 → 5, sand's ripple 7 → 5, snow's sparkle 16 → 12. **Measured** (1600×1200, seed 12345, spring, whole-map 1:1, luma band-passed to 13–39 px per cycle — the bake's own perceived roughness): **rock 5.45 → 6.01** and hills 4.34 → 4.61 on highland, **rock 4.93 → 5.50** on continental, while grass barely moved (4.06 → 4.09 and 3.25 → 3.29) — the rough materials gained inside the band and the smooth ones did not. Whole-map bake cost **1.28 → 1.44 s** / **1.35 → 1.49 s** for one extra noise octave per land pixel, so a 256 px chunk on the shipping chunked path goes ~27 ms → ~30 ms. One case in `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` pins both halves of the rule: every material owns at least one octave inside the band, and `bandDetail` returns the same value when the world step doubles.

- **New: the rain shadow — a mountain range finally has a wet side and a dry side** — moisture was a noise field plus a **symmetric** altitude penalty, so both flanks of a range dried by the same amount: a range had no windward side, no leeward side, and dry ground could only come from the noise itself (which is also why a map's arid ground tended to sit on its coast rather than behind its hills). `terrain/rainShadow.ts` adds the **directional** half of the climate: for every cell it marches **upwind** — a west-south-westerly heading, deliberately not axis-aligned, so a shadow does not read as vertical banding — and records the tallest ground standing between it and the wind, capped so a mountain counts as a wall rather than as an infinite one, and decaying over 48 cells so a leeward plain recovers as it runs on. The result is a 0–1 dryness the moisture pass subtracts before the biome cuts are taken, which is the right order for a reason worth stating: those cuts are **quantiles of the finished field**, so a map keeps its wet/dry *proportions* while their *placement* becomes topographic — the same valley is wooded on the windward side and open on the lee. **Measured** (2560×1920, seed 12345): at matched mid-altitude, shaded ground reads **0.12–0.18** moisture against **0.32–0.36** in the open (arabia 0.320 → 0.122, continental 0.357 → 0.180, black_forest 0.698 → 0.448), with a correlation of **−0.49…−0.65** between the barrier and the moisture on every preset; eight cells downwind of the tallest ridges the ground is drier than the windward cell (coastal 0.303 → 0.224, islands 0.418 → 0.296, scandinavia 0.375 → 0.315). The field is pure — elevation in, dryness out, no RNG and no dependence on iteration order — and each preset's climate stays itself (arabia 68 % dry / 17 % wet, oasis 84 / 2, black_forest 1 / 61, meadows 12 / 63). Two cases in `tests/terragen.latitude.test.ts` guard it: the rule on a synthetic wall (lee shaded, windward clear, decay with distance, level ground unshaded, byte-identical on re-run) and the effect through the generator's own moisture field at matched elevation, where the gap a rain shadow exists to make is **0.213** against a bar of 0.08.

- **New: biome placement follows latitude — a map's north and south edges are cold and its middle row is the equator, from the parameterization in Choroś & Topolski's extended-Voronoi terrain paper** — the generator chose every biome from a per-map climate constant, so `arabia` was hot *everywhere* and `scandinavia` was cold *everywhere*: the same climate in every direction, which is why a generated valley read as a scatter of biome patches rather than as terrain with a north and a south. Choroś & Topolski (*Parameterized and Dynamic Generation of an Infinite Virtual Terrain with Various Biomes using Extended Voronoi Diagram*, J.UCS 22:6, 2016) parameterize a biome by **position** — a latitude axis — rather than by a constant, and that single idea is what ported. **The map's y axis is now its latitude:** `latitudeCooling` in `terrain/terragen.ts` is a **triangular wave** over the cell row — 0 on the middle row (the equator), maximal at both edges (the poles) — so the cool zones are symmetric about the middle instead of biased to one end. **It is additive, and that is why the presets had to be re-based:** latitude only removes heat, and it is subtracted *before* the long-standing `× 1.1` amplifier, so the equator row reads exactly that preset's authored `temperature` and every other row is colder. That makes `temperature` **the equator's air temperature** — and a preset only has zonation at all if it clears `COLD_TEMPERATURE` (0.3) *after* the elevation penalty (0.7 per unit above sea level), because otherwise `classifyTile` calls the whole map cold. Measured, `scandinavia` at its old 0.18 cleared that on **no row at all**: 0 % grass, 0 % forest, 29–41 % dark forest, and latitude changed nothing. The cool presets are therefore re-based so their equator is genuinely temperate — `scandinavia` **0.18 → 0.56**, `highland` **0.40 → 0.62**, and smaller lifts on the rest (`arabia` 0.75 → 0.82, `black_forest` 0.52 → 0.66, `coastal` 0.62 → 0.70, `islands` 0.78 → 0.80, `meadows` 0.60 → 0.68, `oasis` 0.90 → 0.92, `rivers` 0.55 → 0.64, `continental` 0.65 → 0.70), with the rule recorded beside the table in `PRESET_SETTINGS`. **That re-basing is where the zonation comes from, and it is measured:** `scandinavia` equator band **0.08 → 0.46** against 0.23 top / 0.38 bottom, warm land **0 % → 34 %** with cold land 63 % → **29 %**; `highland` 0.22 → 0.45 and warm land **7 % → 33 %**; `arabia` 0.49 → 0.57 at 54 % warm; `continental` 0.53 → 0.59 at 43 % warm. `scandinavia` stays the coldest preset by a clear margin — it is boreal through its **latitude bands and its moisture**, not by being frozen on every row. **The band edge is drifted by coherent noise** (`hash2`, not a value-noise lattice, so the offset brings no lattice of its own) because a raw triangular wave draws perfectly straight horizontal climate lines — the artefact the paper adds detail to avoid; the noise is seeded, so two seeds do not share one set of climate lines and one seed still reproduces its own exactly. **One source of truth with two readers:** the parameter is applied *inside* the `temperature` field rather than at the classifier, so `terrainGrid.classifyTile` (the tile a settler walks on) and the per-pixel ground bake both get the zonation and cannot disagree about it — `WorldMap.temperature` documents the axis. `LATITUDE_SPAN` is **0.3**, deliberately modest: at 0.55 the polar rows land on the `clamp` floor, where the gradient is lost and a whole edge reads as one flat band (measured, `continental` warm-biome share 6 % at 0.55 against 11 % at 0.3, with 9 % of cells pinned). **No limit is left papered over:** the re-base is what opens the zonation, and it is a deliberate change to what these presets mean — `scandinavia` is now boreal through its latitude bands and its moisture rather than by being frozen on every row, which is the intended read but is a tuning decision the owner should know was taken. Measured across two seeds the re-based presets hold their character: `scandinavia` 34 % warm / 29 % cold land at seed 12345 and 56 % / 20 % at seed 777, `highland` 33 % / 17 % and 67 % / 11 %, against `oasis` and `arabia` staying hot (53–75 % warm, 15–17 % cold). The paper's infinite-terrain machinery — its wrapping `mod(2S)` zone cycle, quad LOD layers and spiral draw order — is deliberately **not** ported: Wilderfolk's map is a finite `width`×`height` rectangle, so there is no "last zone" to wrap from. Four cases in `tests/terragen.latitude.test.ts` pin the gradient, its symmetry, its non-saturation, its seed dependence and determinism. Gates: `npm run test:all` **240 files / 1475 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**, `tsc` clean on both projects, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0.

- **Developer: the browser smoke tier is rewritten from scratch on Playwright, and it gates green again** — the old `scripts/browser-smoke.mjs` was a *partial reconstruction* (its own header recorded 1,562 of 2,696 lines re-mined after the 2026-09-20 tree revert, with `VIEWPORT_MATRIX`, `runViewportMatrix`, `runPanelPass` and `runSettlerWalkThrough` present but **uncalled** and the acceptance checklist unrecoverable), and it had been failing on its own flow rather than on the game: it clicked the intro at ~1.2 s, when the intro is a **timed cinematic whose call-to-action is not yet enabled**, and it treated "found a button" as "clicked a button" — so *HUD never appeared*, *no `#camera-zoom-preset` control* and *the simulation is not ticking* were all cascades of that one step (the identical three failures are in `tmp/shots/report.json` dated 21-9, three days before this session). The rewrite drives the build through the real player path — boot → the intro's first **enabled** CTA → settlement screen → `Settle the valley` (including the armed-confirmation branch a save triggers) → gameplay HUD → tutorial card → live valley — and proves each step from the page: the ground actually painted (distinct colours, mean luma, void ratio sampled from the map canvas), the sim actually ticking (the HUD clock, with a 25 s window because a soft-rasterising browser is slow), the zoom control exercised through `#camera-zoom-preset` with its live readout checked, and the console/page/request gates. Two measurements shaped it. **(1) The pause is not the pace.** The guided first-spring overlay freezes the world on purpose, and the HUD button's `aria-label` flips to "running" while the simulation worker still reports `Paused` — a Space press (the shipped shortcut) is what actually resumes it. **(2) The default viewport is 900×600, not 1600×900.** A GPU-less headless browser soft-rasterises every canvas path, and a CPU profile of one valley frame put **92 % of main-thread self time in native `fill`/`stroke`/`restore`** with individual frames of 5–6 s; at that size the tick counter did not advance inside 21 s even with the world resumed, while at 900×600 the same build ticks normally (tick 24 → 27, clock 08:00 → 09:00 in 21 s). Per-frame performance stays another tier's job. **Verified: three consecutive runs, `exit=0`, `verdict=pass`**, and `tmp/shots/report.json` now records the check table. Dependency: **`playwright-core`** (a devDependency) drives the Chrome already on the machine — no bundled browser download. What the rewrite deliberately drops, because it was uncalled or unrecoverable, is listed in the script header and in `docs/plans/browser-testing-evaluation.md`.

- **New: rivers are generated from hydrology — the SIGGRAPH 2013 model — so a river follows a real drainage network and widens with the land that feeds it** — the generator used to pick river sources by an elevation score and then walk a *gradient* with a meander bend, which meant a "river" was a path and not a network: nothing knew how much land drained through a point, two paths could cross, the walk needed a stagnation bail-out for when the gradient flattened on a depression, the channel's width was a function of how far along the path it had travelled (`step / 200`), and every river cell was clamped to `seaLevel - 0.01` with a `seaLevel + 0.04` ring around it — **a mountain stream was cut down to sea level, a canyon through a hillside, and that plate plus its ring is what made every river edge a terrace on the 16 px lattice**. The port is Génevaux et al., *Terrain Generation Using Procedural Models Based on Hydrology*, and it keeps the paper's own chain: **route the water, then read the river off the area that drains through it.** A new owner module, `terrain/hydrology.ts`, does three passes — **fill sinks** by priority flood (a binary heap, ties broken by cell index, so the result is bit-identical run to run), **D8 steepest descent plus flat resolution**, and **accumulation** in one sorted sweep. Sources are now high ground where *little land drains through* (the paper's springs, not its river mouths), paths follow the network downhill so two rivers meet where their catchments meet instead of crossing, and the channel's width comes from the paper's mean-flow law `φ = 0.42·A^0.69` (§5.1, after Dunne & Leopold) through the standard hydraulic-geometry width law `w ∝ φ^0.5`, so `w ∝ A^0.345` — a headwater is 0.3 of a preset's channel width and the map's trunk river 2.4 of it, with the map's own largest catchment as the normaliser so every preset keeps the width it asks for. The carve itself is the paper's river primitive, `h(p) = u_z(p) + δ(d(p))`: the channel is cut below **its own** surface (depth scales with the channel's width, not with sea level) and the profile meets the banks through the replace operator's compact-support weight instead of stopping at a ring. **Two defects were found by measuring while building it, and both are now pinned by tests.** The first: with the fill alone, **14–27 % of cells had no lower neighbour and the longest path from any cell was ~30 cells** — the network was in fragments and every river drawn on it was a stub, because filling a pit turns it into a *flat* and no flat cell has a strictly-lower neighbour; the fix is the flat-resolution sweep, and the "inverted uphill guard" that came with it closed a cycle that made **every walk traverse the whole grid** (measured: 19 200 steps) until the guard was corrected. The second: using the paper's crest term `q_z = max(a_z, b_z, c_z) + λ·d` as an *upper bound* on ground that already exists cuts a bench as wide as the bank run into any slope a river crosses — it swallowed ridgelines, and it starved the tiles one farm fixture depends on; the banks now give up a **bounded** share of the channel depth and fade out, so the hill's own relief is the valley wall. **Measured after:** interior dead ends **14–27 % → 0**, longest drainage walk **~30 → 132–205 cells**, catchment p50 3 with a trunk of **6 190–12 517 cells** against a mean of ~4 (that tail is what makes width-from-area meaningful), rivers per preset unchanged (1–5, and `oasis` still zero by design), generation **+12–158 ms** for the two passes on a 2560×1920 map, and the same seed still produces the identical world (`workerMainThread.parity` and the determinism case in `terragen.riverGeneration.test.ts` both pass). Six cases now guard it — network connectivity and acyclicity, accumulation downstream, and the width law's monotonicity and clamp — and the ground bake keeps its 3×3-smoothed height field, which was added for the old plates and is still worth having for a steep channel. One threshold moved on evidence: the alpine-lawn seam in `groundLook.bands.test.ts` reads **0.08 % → 0.28 %** on highland, because a carved valley really does step at its walls; the ceiling is 0.5 % and the systematic mean gate is untouched, so the defect that test exists for (~100 % lawn) still cannot pass it.

- **New: Teraforge's weather is integrated — the effect registry, camera-parallax cloud and fog layers, and the daylight tint** — the visuals had been reimplemented Wilderfolk-style in `renderer/weather.ts` (Teraforge's ripples, fog bands and snow cover, inside a hard-coded `if`-chain keyed on `state.weather`), so what shipped was Teraforge's *look* without Teraforge's *architecture*, and three of its effects had no equivalent at all. `renderer/weather.ts` is now the ported system: **`registerWeather({ id, label, icon, create })`** with a `weatherRegistry`, and each effect owning **`tint(frame)` / `update(dt, frame)` / `draw(g, frame)`** against a camera-aware `WeatherFrame`. Three consequences worth naming. **(1) Motion is frame-rate independent** — every effect advanced by fixed per-frame increments (`b.x += b.sp * 0.01`, `r.t += 0.03`) before, so weather ran at double speed on a 120 Hz display; `update(dt, …)` fixes that, with `dt` clamped to 100 ms so a backgrounded tab cannot teleport every particle. **(2) The clouds layer is new, and cloud and fog drift with the camera.** Teraforge's soft `multiply` cloud puffs are ported and *composed* into rain, storm, fog and drought rather than registered as a plugin — Wilderfolk has no `Clouds` weather state, and a plugin nothing can select is dead code. Both layers now take a parallax factor against `camX`/`camY` (`CLOUD_PARALLAX`/`FOG_PARALLAX`), which is why `drawWeather` takes the snapshot instead of just `state.weather`. **(3) `daylightTint` is ported** — Teraforge's hour-based daylight table, of which this module applies only the *temperature cast* (drought → summer heat haze, snow → winter cold) between 08:00 and 16:00; the dusk and night terms stay unwired, because Wilderfolk's day/night lighting overlay is disabled on purpose (`renderer/overlay.ts`) and a weather port is not the place to re-enable it. **Three deliberate deviations, each because Wilderfolk's rules outrank Teraforge's code:** randomness comes from `getPresentationRng('weatherFx')`, never `Math.random` (Teraforge's `rnd()` is seeded here — `src/` may not call `Math.random`, and `tests/simRng.test.ts` enforces it), so weather stays deterministic per seed and out of the simulation's streams; **`WEATHER_CONFIGS` stays the one owner of the numbers** (colour, overlay alpha, particle count per state) instead of Teraforge's hard-coded tints; and **`drought`, which Teraforge does not have, is authored in its idiom** (warm dust haze plus the table's brown veil). Two smaller corrections fall out: **fog's declared veil now actually draws** (the table has always said `overlayAlpha: 0.28`, but the old band path returned before it was laid down), and snow's tint brightens with Teraforge's `screen` blend while the veil states keep `source-over`, which is what Wilderfolk's alphas were tuned for. The simulation is untouched — `updateWeather`, the farm multiplier and the dormant hostile-weather switch all behave exactly as before. Gates: `npm run test:types` exit 0, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `tests/weatherConsequences.test.ts` 13 passed (3 new: one plugin per weather state, every effect updates and draws a frame against a stub 2D context, and the ported daylight terms), `tests/hostileWeather.disabled.test.ts` 3 passed, `npm run test:standard` 239 files / 1465 passed / 2 skipped.

- **Fixed: the ground no longer freezes the frame — a chunk is baked for what the camera can see, at the resolution the screen can show, and a routine build stops re-baking the world** — the per-pixel Whittaker ground had been baked **whole-map, synchronously, on the main thread**: **3.2–3.4 s** on a Medium map and **7.7–8.0 s** on Large, and because the cache key carried `getTerrainRevision()` — which `buildingPlacementActions` bumps when it clears a footprint — **clearing forest to place a building re-baked the entire world**. It is now **chunked by what the camera can see** (`renderer/terrain.ts`: one canvas per visible viewport chunk, margin one chunk, the two map-wide fields built once per map and shared), and two measurements decided the shape of it. **(1) The chunk size is the size of the hitch you feel.** A chunk is baked synchronously the frame it is first needed, so 1024² chunks cost ~0.43 s each *and* bake many times the visible ground — the grid is chunk-aligned and keeps a chunk of margin, so the first frame on Huge measured **16.8 Mpx (11.0 s) for a 0.7 Mpx view**, sixteen times more ground than the camera could show. At **256²** a chunk is ~27 ms and the same frame is 3.1 Mpx (2.0 s). **(2) The bake resolution has to follow the zoom.** Screen px per world px *is* the camera zoom, so `groundWorldStepForZoom` bakes 1 world px per canvas px from 1× up and 2 from the 0.5× overview — at the overview the old path still baked 1:1 and measured **28.3 Mpx / 17.0 s** for one frame on Huge. First frame after both, same window (1600×900) and camera position: **Medium 3.34 → 1.87 s · Large 8.24 → 1.97 s · Huge 10.97 → 2.01 s**, and at the 0.5× overview **Medium 3.24 → 0.90 s · Large 7.87 → 1.91 s · Huge 17.00 → 2.80 s**. Every size is now sharper *and* faster than the whole-map bake it replaced (`Huge` was 4.1 s at half resolution before any of this). **The stale-chunk key is gone:** the bake reads the generated fields and never the sparse `overrides` layer, so the revision counter is out of the key — that is what made a build freeze the frame — and the world dims went in, because the same seed and preset at a different map size is a different map. A latent mis-draw went with it: chunks were drawn at their **canvas** size, which is only equal to the world rect while the bake is 1:1 — wrong the moment it sub-samples, so the cache entry now carries the world rect it covers. Five cases in `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` pin the whole arrangement, including that four 256 px chunks at a world step of 2 reproduce the matching crop of a whole-map bake **pixel for pixel**, and that a cleared footprint changes the tile projection without moving a single ground pixel.

- **Fixed: the runtime import cycle, the dead de-speckle branch and a stray 48 MB render** — three recorded findings closed. `placementUtils → terrainGrid` was a **real runtime cycle of two** (`terrainGrid.isTileBuildable` calls `placementUtils.isUnbuildableTerrainType`, while `placementUtils` needs `terrainGrid.tileAt`), and the comment above it claimed the edge was type-only; the "what a terrain type is" pair (`isUnbuildableTerrainType` / `isWaterTerrainType` and their sets) now lives in the leaf module **`terrain/terrainTraits.ts`** — the pattern `OWNERSHIP_OVERVIEW.md` already endorses for `movementSteering` — and the six importers (terrainGrid, terragen, terrainGen, renderer/grid, renderer/weather, placementUtils) read it from there. `npm run audit:deps` now reports **no runtime import cycles** (was *"cycle of 2"*). In `terragen.ts`, the de-speckle pass carried `if (self === B.river) continue;` although `B.river` is never assigned by that function — rivers are the `riverDist` field, not a biome label — so the guard could not fire and is gone; and `buildWaterField`'s output is named `coverage` rather than `dist`, because a 1-to-0 channel coverage is what the tile projection, the biome pass and the bake actually read and nothing takes a distance of it. The 48 MB `map-green-fields-201.png` (a 6144×4608 generator render) moved out of the repository root into `tmp/`, where scratch belongs and where that same artefact already lives.

- **New: the ground finally paints the landscape the classifier describes — mountains are stone, cold crests are snow, water reads as water, and the 16 px lattice no longer shows through** — an audit of the new terrain stack found the generator healthy (every preset reaches rock/snow/hills, deterministic, 0.1–0.2 s) and the **per-pixel ground bake** the thing that looked wrong. **The defect with teeth: the bake coloured land on *absolute* elevation above sea (`e − seaLevel`) while `classifyTile` classifies on the *normalised* land range (`(e − seaLevel) / (1 − seaLevel)`).** On any preset with `seaLevel ≥ 0.12` that puts the whole alpine half of the ramp out of reach — measured on a 2560×1920 scandinavia map the field tops out at **1.077** while the old ramp only turned white at **1.176**, so **0.0 % of the land could ever paint snow** while 1.0 % of its tiles were `Snow`; continental needed `e ≥ 1.143` against a maximum of 1.159, and its 6.3 % `Rocky` + 2.4 % `Mountains` tiles were painted lawn green. The bands are now the classifier's own, exported from `terrainGrid` (`LAND_BANDS` 0.5 / 0.62 / 0.72 / 0.84, `COLD_TEMPERATURE`, `BEACH_BAND_OF_LAND_RANGE`, `MOISTURE_DRY_BELOW_FOREST`) and read by both — one source of truth for where the mountains start, for the walkable tile and for the eye. **Then the four things that make a landscape read, each measured rather than guessed.** *Light:* the old hillshade only darkened, which reads as grime rather than as light — lit faces now warm and shaded faces cool — and the shading reads a **precomputed cell-resolution slope field** (`slopeX`/`slopeY`, one pass per map) rather than differencing raw cells, because a raw difference is constant across a single cell and draws the ground as a mosaic of 16 px squares (caught on a 1:1 crop of the bake); the cast shadow is softer and cooler and the slope rim weaker. *Water:* the shelf and deep arms **did not meet at the shelf edge** (`[12,45,90]` against `[34,83,134]`), which drew a hard dark ring inside every shelf plus a bright cyan rim; there is now one continuous depth ramp, a pale foam line at the waterline, bathymetric contour bands where un-warped cloud noise used to read as dirt, and a river whose core is deeper than its shoulder. *Edges:* the generator's river carve writes whole-cell plates (channel at `seaLevel − 0.01`, banks at `seaLevel + 0.04`), so the waterline landed hard on the 16 px lattice and every carved pool was a staircase — the ground art now reads a **3×3-smoothed height** (classification keeps the raw field, so walkability does not move) and a pixel-scale jitter displaces the coastline and the river-bank threshold on smooth contours. *Material and season:* ground detail moved off the nearest cell's **biome label** — which put every biome boundary on the lattice, and which the old per-pixel static had been hiding — onto the smooth fields, at 3–25 px octaves instead of the old 1.25 px and 0.4 px shimmer, and the season shift is now weighted by vegetation: stone no longer turns brown in summer or grey in winter, and winter settles snow **with height** instead of washing the map flat. Net effect measured on the bake: mean green-minus-red over alpine tiles **0.58 / 1.03 / 0.79** (scandinavia / highland / continental) where the old ramp made them woodland green by ~45. **Cost:** 2.22–2.34 s for the whole-map 2048×1536 bake against a 2.00–2.10 s baseline (**+7…+12 %**, inside the 15 % budget the plan set) — the extra passes are paid for by replacing four bilinear elevation samples with two samples of the precomputed slope field. **Map sizes, measured** (whole-map bake, seed 12345, continental, the shape the shipping renderer still uses): Medium 2560×1920 **3.2–3.4 s** / 18.8 MB at 1:1 · Large 4096×3072 **7.7–8.0 s** / 48 MB at 1:1 · Huge 6144×4608 **4.1 s** / 27 MB at 1:2. **`FULL_BAKE_PIXEL_BUDGET` was 12 M and Large is 12.58 M, so it missed by 4.6 %** — the *larger* map baked at half resolution and rendered visibly softer than Medium at *half* its bake cost, with its coastlines aliasing back into the 16 px steps the jitter exists to remove (the jitter was ~3 px wavelength, sampled under Nyquist at `PX = 2`). Both are addressed: the budget is now 13 M so Large bakes 1:1, and the coast/bank jitter moved to an **~8 px wavelength**, which reads as a meander at 1:1 instead of a torn edge and survives half-resolution sampling for Huge. Huge still bakes 1:2, because 1:1 there is a 113 MB surface. **Known limit, unchanged and recorded:** the renderer still bakes the whole map in one synchronous call (`buildTerrainCache` never passes the `viewRect` the baker supports), so that 7.7–8.0 s is a **frozen main thread** — and the cache key includes `getTerrainRevision()`, so clearing forest for a building re-bakes the world (3.2 s on Medium, 8 s on Large). That is roadmap Theme 1's open item 1, and it is the fix that makes this budget question disappear: a viewport bake is 1:1 by construction, because `PX` is decided by the area actually baked. Gates: `npm run test:types` exit 0, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `tests/groundLook.bands.test.ts` 3/3 (alpine is stone, a cold crest is snow, the bake is deterministic **and a viewport bake matches the same crop of the whole map** — the invariant a chunked bake will be built on).

- **Fixed: the Teraforge overhaul actually runs — the four-layer tile model was half-migrated, so `generateWorldMap` threw on every call and the valley never generated at all** — the terrain pass had removed `WorldMap.tiles`, the single 10 px `TerrainTile[][]` the whole game read, and left **25 files and 83 type errors** behind it: `terrainGen.generateWorldMap` called its own camp-clearing helpers with a grid that no longer existed (`forEachTileInRadius` dereferenced `undefined`), so **every** `initGame`, every new settlement and every save reload threw `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined`. `npm run build`, `npm run test:types` and 29 test cases were red. **The representation is now finished rather than reverted**, as the roadmap specified: a new owner, `terrain/terrainGrid.ts`, is the one reader of the four-layer grid — `tileAt`/`tileTypeAt` project the 10 px tile from the L2 continuous fields (memoised per map, `undefined` off-map exactly like the old `tiles[ty]?.[tx]`), `setTileOverride`/`patchTile` write the sparse override layer the camp and forest clearing use, `isTileWalkable`/`isTileBuildable` answer from the L0 path grid and the placement owner's own unbuildable set, `rebakeTerrainGrids` re-derives both grids after an edit, and `terrainReader`/`tileGridSnapshot` serve the bulk and test cases. Every consumer was migrated to it — `pathfinding`, `placementUtils`, `terrainSystems`, `worldGen`, `tickLayerSystems`, `buildingPlacementActions`, `audio.workDetect`, `saveLoad`, `terrainAtlas`, `terrainLayer`, the grid/weather/Pixi overlays, `MiniMap` and `MapSetupScreen` — and the 11 test files that hand-built `{ tiles, width, height, seed, preset }` maps now build real ones through a shared `src/test/worldMapFixtures.ts`. **Three real defects fell out of the migration:** the camp clearing wrote to a grid that no longer existed, so the pioneers' clearing was silently dead (it now writes overrides and rebakes the grids, so the cleared ground is walkable *and* buildable); the L0 grid was sized `floor(height / 10)` against tiles of `ceil(height / 10)`, so a 4096×3072 map reserved decor beyond the end of the grid — the reservation was dropped silently by the typed array and a big rock sat on a cell the pathfinder still called open; and `saveLoad.restoreWorldMapFromSave` tested `parsed.worldMap.tiles`, which a `JSON.parse` payload can never have, so the branch was dead and a full save was always regenerated from seed. Gates: `npm run test:types` exit 0, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0, `npm run test:standard` **266 files / 1459 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**, and the 360-day oracle `npm run test:full-year` healthy — **0 invariant violations, 0 stalled ticks, 0 worker fallbacks, 0 serialization spikes** over 25 920 ticks.

- **New: the Teraforge generator is tuned for the landscape it was supposed to make — real mountain ranges, real wet/dry regions, and biomes that follow the climate chart** — the overhaul's engine ran, but the valley it produced was flat, uniformly green and mostly canopy, which is what the owner saw and reported ("the old gen was ugly", then a screenshot with two regions circled for refinement). **Four measured causes, each fixed at its source.** (1) **The height field used only a third of its own range.** Min/max normalisation stretched two outliers across 0–1 and left the bulk of the land in `h ∈ [0.07, 0.32]`, so the biome bands — `rock` at 75 % of the land range, `snow` at 88 % — were **unreachable**: a generated continental map measured **0.1 % rock and 0 % snow**, with no ridgelines to read at all. `spreadHeights` now maps the `[p0.5, p99.5]` quantiles across 0–1 and derives the new sea level from the quantile the old one sat at, so the relief is opened **without moving the coastline or changing the water fraction by a single cell**. (2) **The moisture field was one climate, not a landscape**, and the preset's own climate bias never reached it. A measured map put **67 % of its land above the grass→forest cut and 0 % below the dry cut**, so an arid preset could not produce desert and every preset looked like the same damp field. The bias is now applied, the fBm's narrow spread is amplified against its *measured* range (0.42 → 0.65 at the quartiles, not 0–1, which is what saturated the wet presets on the first attempt), and altitude dries high ground out. (3) **Grassland was a catch-all instead of a biome.** `classifyTile` treated it as the fallback for every unmatched cell, which is why one threshold flipped a map between 40 % grass / 1 % forest and 1 % grass / 47 % forest. It now follows the biome chart the overhaul is modelled on — grassland is the dry-temperate biome, forest above it, taiga where the air is cold, and elevation as the outer gate so the alpine bands are actually reachable — with the two moisture cuts **derived per map from that map's own precipitation distribution**, which is what lets an arid and a wet preset share a composition while keeping their own character. (4) **Rivers were drawn with hard dark outlines.** The cast-shadow pass darkened water, and a carved channel sits far below its banks, so every river bend's inside corner was painted near-black; water is now excluded from that pass (Teraforge damps its hillshade over water for the same reason but never guarded its cast shadow), and the river blend is curved rather than clamped so the bank reads as a bank. **The last of the tile-era look is gone too:** `bakeTerrainDecor`'s water-rim loop, snow mounds, sand ripples and hash-scattered ground props drew a square grid over the smooth per-pixel ground and have been deleted, `stampMountainPeaks` went with them (it requested `/sprites/mountains/{45,135,225,315}.png`, a directory that **does not exist**, so it never drew anything), and the L3 decor layer now applies Teraforge's per-prop `tint` so a field of identical props stops reading as a stamped pattern. Measured across six presets after the pass: continental 36 % grass / 20 % forest / 12 % hills, arabia 23 / 27 / 17 with 11 % rock, highland 33 % forest with **10 % mountain**, meadows 21 / 30 / 19, black_forest 20 / 39 / 20, rivers 28 / 24 / 11 — each preset legibly its own landscape instead of the same green field. **Honest limit, recorded not hidden: the terrain cell was still 64 px at that point**, so a river's edge and a coastline were quantised to that lattice. That is now fixed — see the next entry — and the reason it was *not* fixed by simply lowering the constant is recorded there: doing it alone turned three tests red, including a real worker-vs-main determinism divergence.

- **New: the ground is smooth — the water layer got its own finer lattice, the terrain lattice dropped from 64 px to 16 px, and biome edges are dithered** — the owner circled two regions of a generated map (a flat textureless plateau and a stair-stepped river with hard dark tile outlines) and asked for the biomes to merge beautifully. Researched against how map generators normally solve this, and applied in the order that makes it safe. **One resolution cannot serve both layers:** the climate/biome field is smooth and content at 64 px, but the water mask is where the eye sees structure, and at 64 px a channel and its banks are whole cells, so the waterline is a staircase. `WATER_CELL` (16 px) now rasterises rivers and the shoreline on **its own** lattice — rivers carry widths in biome-cell units, so they are converted to world pixels and the falloff is computed in world space — and `WorldMap` gained `waterCols`/`waterRows` so the tile projection and the per-pixel bake each sample the field in its own cell coordinates. Only then was `TERRAIN_CELL` dropped **64 → 16**: with nothing left depending on it agreeing with the water mask, the change reclassifies nothing a fixture pins. **The lattice went from 40 × 30 cells to 160 × 120** on a 2560×1920 map, so a coastline, a lake edge and a carved river valley are curves now, not staircases. **The order is the whole point, and it was learned the hard way:** lowering `TERRAIN_CELL` first turned three tests red — `workerMainThread.parity` diverged between the worker and the main thread on the new map (a real, tiny float divergence the old map never exposed, worth its own defect report), plus two fixtures that pin terrain by cell coordinate. Splitting the water lattice off first is what made the resolution change free. **Biome boundaries are dithered:** `classifyTile` takes a coherent zero-mean dither — the cell's own elevation field, already in cache, so no second noise evaluation — that shifts every band at once, so two biomes interlock along a ragged edge instead of meeting on a lattice line. **And the water reads as water:** the per-pixel shader gained a **wet-sand tideline** band (the shore now darkens into the waterline and dries inland instead of stepping once) and tighter depth bands, so a shallow shelf stays a shelf — the previous spread darkened every lake to ocean navy within a few pixels of shore and made each one a flat disc. Cost, measured: whole-map ground bake **2.3–2.5 s** at 2048×1536 (was 1.8–1.9 s) and generation 21 ms at 2560×1920. Gates: `test:types` clean, `lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, **`test:standard` 266 files / 1459 passed, 2 skipped, 0 failed**. The remaining limit — the whole-map bake's cost at the Teraforge spec map sizes — is recorded in the roadmap with the fix (viewport-chunk the bake, the lazy pattern `bakeTerrainLayer` already uses).

### Teraforge integration — generation, tile model and presentation

- **New: the Teraforge terrain overhaul lands — real relief, living rivers, per-pixel ground, and elevation-aware pathfinding** — the generation, renderer and pathfinder from `TERAFORGE.md` are now the shipping build. **Generation** (`src/game/terrain/` + `terrainGen.ts`) replaces the sine-noise world with Teraforge's domain-warped fBm + ridged mountain belts (two criss-crossing ranges so peaks form chains, not mounds), gradient-walker rivers with meander, width growth and **auto-detected waterfalls**, Whittaker biomes classified by elevation × moisture × temperature, and the L0 10 px path grid + L1 20 px build grid — all deterministic from one seed. The map chooser now offers Teraforge's **ten presets** (Arabia, Black Forest, Coastal, Islands, Highland, Scandinavia, Meadows, Oasis, Rivers, Continental) and each card renders a **live generated thumbnail** of that preset's real terrain. **Rendering** (`renderer/whittakerTerrain.ts`) bakes the ground per-pixel — smooth Whittaker gradients, hillshading, cast shadows, slope edges, per-biome texture, river distance fields and underwater depth — instead of the painted tile atlas. **Pathfinding** (`pathfinding.ts`) is now elevation-aware: A* reads the L0 occupancy grid and adds a slope cost per step, with a single-tile elevation jump past the cliff threshold impassable, so settlers prefer gentle routes and route around bluffs. **Weather** (`renderer/weather.ts`) gains Teraforge's rain ground-ripples, accumulating snow cover and drifting fog bands. Six old terrain-engine tests were removed and the four non-terrain tests that named removed presets were re-pointed. Gates: `tsc` clean, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0.

- **Fixed: a save whose *contents* are unusable is refused by name, and a settler's name no longer depends on which file loaded first — the two defects a read-only audit of simulation trustworthiness found that could reach a live world** — the pass asked five questions of the simulation (does one seed reproduce one world; is a failed tick a true undo; do the worker and save boundaries carry every field; are the invariants actually enforced; is a corrupt payload refused) and answered four of them with the code and the gates that already hold. **The one with teeth: the load boundary validated the world's containers and never their contents.** `findUnrestorableField` checked `resources`, `storageMax`, `lifetimeStats` and five scalars, and then the restore built each element by default-and-spread and **cast** it — so a payload that parsed JSON and matched the version gate could put `x: "nope"`, `age: "30"` or `type: "Dragon"` into the live world, where `computeWildlifeCounts` only warned about the unknown type and the invariant that catches a non-finite position is a dev-build pulse once per colony day. The next save then wrote the corruption back, making it permanent. The scan is now element-level (`entities[i].x`, `buildings[i].type`, …) with the map bound on `x`/`y`, `type` mandatory because it is the one key whose absence is not a shape this build can use, and every other key optional because the restore defaults it — and the refusal detail now names the field, so the player is told which one rather than that *something* is wrong. `tests/saveLoad.elementShape.test.ts` (9 cases) corrupts one field of a **real** `buildSaveData` payload per case and proves the uncorrupted payload still loads, so the refusals are about the corruption and not about the fixture. **The second: the sim worker started the census name-files load without awaiting it**, while `pickFrom` indexes whichever pool is installed at that instant — so a birth, immigration or recruit named before the async load landed mapped the same seeded draw to a different element, and a settler's *name* (saved state) varied with chunk timing for one seed. The worker now settles the pool at its message gate (`ensureNamePoolSettled`), which is one message long in practice, cannot wedge the worker because `loadNames` resolves on every path, and is what the main thread already did before `initGame`. Proving it needed a test seam rather than a second module registry: V8 caches dynamically imported modules for the process, so `vi.resetModules()` handed the "fresh" realm the *same* `nameLoader` instance and the differential passed with or without the fix — `resetNamePoolForTest()` stands a realm at its import-time embedded state, and the case was confirmed red before the fix and green after. **Also:** a delta-extraction failure on the command path is no longer reported as a *tick* fault (the host answers a tick-sourced error by tearing the worker down, which it was doing for a command whose world had committed perfectly well), the command half of the rollback contract gained the coverage only the tick half had, and the two module-level id counters (`nextBigNewsId`, `nextEventLogId`), the deliberately non-durable `huntVisuals`/`disasters`, and the wall-clock reason `huntVisuals` keeps a millisecond stamp are now recorded beside the lists they belong to instead of having to be rediscovered. Gates: `tsc` clean on both projects, `npm run test:types` exit 0, `npm run lint` 0 warnings / 0 errors, `npm run test:standard` exit 0.

- **Fixed: the mini-map was not gone, it was underneath the open build catalogue** — reported by the owner as "the minimap is gone ? / it's totally gone". It was neither removed nor failing to render (`GameMapStage` mounts it unconditionally); it was **occluded**. `.game-view-drawer` is `position: absolute; top: 0.75rem; bottom: 0.75rem; z-index: 30` — an absolutely-positioned flex child takes **no flow space**, so `<main class="map-stage">` still begins at x = 0 and the catalogue is a full-height panel laid over the map's left edge (`left: 0.75rem; width: min(26rem, calc(100vw - 2rem))`), while the mini-map is pinned `absolute bottom-4 left-4` with **no z-index of its own**. The collapsed rail never caused this, which is why it looked like a deletion: `--rail` is `bottom: auto; min-height: 20rem`, top-anchored, so it stops well above the bottom-left corner — only the *open* catalogue reaches it, and `buildPanelOpen` is persisted in `localStorage`, so a player whose catalogue was open never saw the mini-map again. **The repair makes the HUD yield, not the overlay**, because overlaying is the recorded intent ("open a clear, wide destination over the map instead of permanently squeezing the playable world into side columns"): the catalogue's width is published once as `--build-drawer-width` on the shell and read by `.game-view-drawer--build`, and one unlayered rule — `@media (min-width: 721px) { .game-shell:has(.game-view-drawer--build.game-view-drawer--open) .minimap-frame { left: calc(0.75rem + var(--build-drawer-width) + 0.5rem) } }` — slides the mini-map clear while the catalogue is open.
